# 2026 United States House of Representatives elections

For related races, see [2026 United States elections](70357296.md).

The **2026 United States House of Representatives elections** are scheduled to be held on Tuesday, November 3, 2026, as part of the [2026 midterm elections](70357296.md) during President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s second nonconsecutive term. Voters will elect representatives from all 435 [congressional districts](449478.md) across each of the fifty [U.S. states](18618239.md), and five of the six [non-voting delegates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-voting_members_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives) from the [District of Columbia](108956.md) and the inhabited [U.S. territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insular_area).

Special elections are also being held on dates throughout 2026. Numerous other federal, state, and local elections, including [elections to the Senate](65785708.md), will be held on this date. The winners of this election will serve in the [120th United States Congress](462457.md), with seats apportioned among the states based on the [2020 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census) and revised district maps as a result of the [2025–2026 United States redistricting](81439539.md).

Primary elections for all 435 House seats, and five of the six non-voting delegate seats were held starting on March 3, and concluded on September 15, 2026.

## Retirements

### Summary

61 representatives and two non-voting delegates (26 Democrats and 37 Republicans) have announced their retirement, 29 of whom (9 Democrats and 20 Republicans) are retiring to run for other offices. This is the second-most retirements of representatives in a single election cycle in US history next to [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections), which saw 65 total retirements.[^cite_note-5]

## Resignations

Two seats will be left vacant on the day of the general election until the next Congress due to resignations.

### Democratic

1. [Florida 20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida's_20th_congressional_district): [Sheila Cherfilus-McCormick](69186390.md) resigned on April 21, 2026, following money laundering allegations.

   [^cite_note-SCM-70]

### Republican

1. [Texas 23](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas's_23rd_congressional_district): [Tony Gonzales](65756758.md) resigned on April 14, 2026, after admitting to an affair.

   [^cite_note-Gonzales-71]

## Crossover seats

This is a list of congressional seats that voted for one party in the [2024 presidential election](59568546.md) and another in the [2024 House elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections).[^cite_note-89]

## Mid-decade redistricting changes

Main article: [2025–2026 United States redistricting](81439539.md)



States that have undergone mid-decade redistricting prior to the 2026 elections colored by which party is expected to benefit.

| Finalized new map; Democratic gain expected  Voluntary redistricting rejected | Finalized new map; Republican gain expected  Redistricting challenge rejected  New map finalized; later struck down in court |
| --- | --- |



States that have undergone mid-decade redistricting prior to the 2026 elections colored by the reason for redistricting.

| Voluntary redistricting enacted  Voluntary redistricting rejected | Court-ordered/mandatory redistricting  Redistricting challenge rejected   New map finalized; later struck down in court |
| --- | --- |

In the United States, all states with multiple congressional districts are required to revise their district maps following each decennial census to account for population changes. In 2026, most states will use the same districts created in the [redistricting cycle following the 2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_redistricting_cycle), which were first used in the 2022 elections. Maps have changed or would change in several states, often due to legal challenges made based on partisan or racial [gerrymandering](42223515.md).

As of October 2026, several states have seen challenges to their [congressional district](449478.md) maps that were put in place during the [redistricting cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_redistricting_cycle) brought upon by the results of the 2020 census. In [Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Arkansas), a federal court dismissed a case against their congressional map that argued the map did not comply with the [Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act).[^cite_note-97] The [Florida Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Florida) dismissed a challenge regarding the Florida's congressional map, finding a new plurality-Black congressional district would be against federal law.[^cite_note-98]

In [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Georgia), litigation is still ongoing regarding appeals to the newly drawn congressional map that was used in the 2024 elections. However, it was not resolved before the filing deadline for the 2026 Congressional elections.[^cite_note-99] In [Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Louisiana), litigation remained unresolved.[^cite_note-SC-100] In South Carolina, the [South Carolina Supreme Court](6985896.md) ruled that partisan gerrymandering is not in violation of the state's constitution, keeping the maps in place.[^cite_note-\:0-101] In [Texas](29810.md), a trial was held in federal court in [El Paso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Paso,_Texas) to determine whether its congressional map discriminated against Black and Latino voters in violation of the VRA,[^cite_note-TXmap1-102] but the case was paused pending expected new maps in the state.[^cite_note-LULAC-103]

In [Utah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Utah), a partisan gerrymander case resulted in a map which creates a safe Democratic district comprising the majority of [Salt Lake County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_Lake_County) following a district court ruling.[^cite_note-\:4-104] In [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Wisconsin), a challenge to the state's current congressional map, arguing it was an unlawful partisan gerrymander, was dismissed by the [Wisconsin Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Supreme_Court).[^cite_note-autogenerated3-105] A new map was required in [Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Ohio) due to the Congressional Redistricting Procedures Amendment to Section XI of the [state's constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Ohio), resulting in two districts becoming more Republican leaning.[^cite_note-OHnewmap-106]

### Out-of-cycle partisan redistricting efforts

[Governor Greg Abbott](1492735.md) called a [special session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_session) of the [Texas Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Legislature) to redraw its congressional districts, leading to the Texas Legislature passing new maps resulting in 5 new Republican-leaning districts.[^cite_note-tg-107] On November 18, a panel of the [District Court for the Western District of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Western_District_of_Texas) issued a preliminary injunction blocking the new map, putting the former map back in place,[^cite_note-\:5-108] but the Supreme Court later placed an administrative stay on the ruling, allowing the new maps to stand.[^cite_note-TXmap-109] [Governor Ron DeSantis](36729527.md) has expressed interest in redrawing [Florida's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Florida) congressional map, and the Florida House has created a special redistricting committee.[^cite_note-110] [Governor Mike Kehoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Kehoe) of [Missouri](19571.md) called a special session on August 29, 2025, which redrew the solid Democratic [5th district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri's_5th_congressional_district) to a solid Republican district, allowing the GOP to pick up a seat.[^cite_note-\:1-111] However, on September 3, the [Missouri Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Supreme_Court) unanimously ruled that the referendum on the new maps was sufficient and must be put on the ballot, thus leaving the 2022 maps in place for the 2026 elections.[^cite_note-\:6-112] Republican state legislative leaders in [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_North_Carolina) announced a redraw of the state's congressional districts to make the 1st congressional district more Republican leaning.[^cite_note-NCmap-113]

Talks and efforts about redistricting occurred in [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Nebraska), [New Hampshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_New_Hampshire), [Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Indiana), and [Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Kansas), but either failed or did not gain traction.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115][^cite_note-INmap-116][^cite_note-KSmap-117]

Democrats have threatened to retaliate against attempts to mid-decade redistrict for partisan gain and have stated the possibility of redrawing the congressional maps in blue states. In [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_California), the [state legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_State_Legislature) drafted a map eliminating five Republican districts to combat Texas's plan, which was approved by voters. In [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Virginia), members of the [General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_General_Assembly) announced a plan on October 23, 2025 to begin the process to redraw the state's congressional districts before the 2026 elections.[^cite_note-auto-118]

Democratic governor [Wes Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wes_Moore) of [Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Maryland) unsuccessfully pursued redistricting in his state.[^cite_note-MDmap-119] Redistricting has been considered in [Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_Colorado), [New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_New_Jersey), and [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_New_York). Legal and political challenges make redistricting before 2026 highly unlikely.[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122]

| State (linked to summaries below) | Status | Notes | Change in partisanship[^cite_note-change-123] |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D | C | R |  |  |  |
| [Alabama](#Alabama) | New districts enacted | In April 2026, the Supreme Court ruled on *[Louisiana v. Callais](80600781.md)* that stricter scrutiny had to be applied with regard to section 2 of the [Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act). Governor [Kay Ivey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kay_Ivey) was initially reluctant to call a special session after *[Allen v. Milligan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_v._Milligan)*, but was lobbied to do so by the state attorney general and secretary of state, both of whom also filed emergency injunctions to lift the requirements levied on them by the federal courts.[^cite_note-124] The Supreme Court voted 6–3 to lift the stay, paving the way for state legislators to redraw.[^cite_note-125] On May 26, 2026, an Alabama court blocked the new map, arguing that the *[Louisiana v. Callais](80600781.md)* decision does not change the fact that the map intentionally discriminates against African-American voters in violation of the Constitution.[^cite_note-126] On June 2, 2026, the [Supreme Court](31737.md) granted stay on the block, allowing the new map to be implemented.[^cite_note-127] | Steady | Decrease 1 | Increase 1 |
| [Arkansas](#Arkansas) | Districts left in place | In *Christian Ministerial Alliance v. Thurston*, plaintiffs alleged racial gerrymandering diluting the voting power of black voters by splitting [Little Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Rock) into three districts. The case was heard in a federal district court. The panel ruled that it did not constitute a racial gerrymander and dismissed the case.[^cite_note-128] | Steady | Steady | Steady |
| [California](#California) | [New districts enacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_California_proposed_redistricting) | In response to Texas's redistricting, a new map was approved by voters in a special statewide election temporarily permitting an off-cycle redistricting. The state's [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_California) prohibits the [state legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_State_Legislature) from drawing congressional districts, so the map was subject to a 2025 referendum. It was passed by two-thirds of the legislature and over a 64% majority approval by the state's voters for the new districts to take place.[^cite_note-CM-129] | Increase 4 | Decrease 3 | Decrease 1 |
| [Florida](#Florida) | [New districts enacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Florida_redistricting) | In September 2023, a state circuit judge ruled that the map passed by the state legislature discriminated against Black voters, by redrawing the formerly plurality-Black 5th district to no longer have a Black plurality. Florida appealed this ruling and the map was reinstated by an appeals court.[^cite_note-130] In July 2025, the Florida Supreme Court ruled 5–1 to keep the current maps in place, finding that creating a plurality-Black congressional district would violate the federal [Equal Protection Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Protection_Clause).[^cite_note-131]On January 9, 2026, Governor [Ron DeSantis](36729527.md) called for a special session in April redrawing the state's congressional maps with the target of gaining 3 to 5 new Republican seats. However, Florida state law prevents deliberately redrawing congressional maps purely for partisan gain.[^cite_note-132] On February 27, 2026, the [Supreme Court of Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Florida) rejected a petition to block the special session.[^cite_note-133] On April 29, 2026, the maps were approved by the [Florida Legislature](429346.md).[^cite_note-134] On May 4, 2026, Governor DeSantis signed the new map into law.[^cite_note-135] | Increase 1 | Decrease 4 | Increase 3 |
| [Indiana](#Indiana) | Districts left in place | On November 18, 2025, the Indiana State Senate voted 29–19 against meeting in December with their House colleagues to discuss redistricting.[^cite_note-136] Despite this, Governor [Mike Braun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Braun) stated that he would "explore all options" to enable redistricting, in response to pressure from President Trump. On November 25, the state senate announced it would vote on a potential proposal approved by the state house.[^cite_note-137] After passing in the state house, the state senate rejected the proposal 31–19.[^cite_note-INmap-116] | Steady | Steady | Steady |
| [Kansas](#Kansas) | Districts left in place | Republican Kansas state legislators announced a plan to call a special session to redraw the 3rd congressional district, but lacked the necessary support in the [House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_House_of_Representatives).[^cite_note-KSmap-117] | Steady | Steady | Steady |
| [Louisiana](#Louisiana) | New districts enacted | In April 2026, the Supreme Court ruled on *[Louisiana v. Callais](80600781.md)* that stricter scrutiny had to be applied with regard to section 2 of the [Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act). The state following the ruling requested the Court strike down the lower court ruling to have two black majority districts, which it proceeded to do. The state government has pushed to move back the primary dates in light of the court requiring redistricting.[^cite_note-138] On May 29, 2026, Governor [Jeff Landry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Landry) signed a new map into law, removing one of the two plurality-Black, Democratic-controlled districts.[^cite_note-139] | Decrease 1 | Steady | Increase 1 |
| [Maryland](#Maryland) | Districts left in place | In November 2025, Governor [Wes Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wes_Moore) announced the creation of the Redistricting Advisory Commission which began the process of redistricting the congressional lines to make the 1st district more Democratic. The plan has received support from State House Speaker [Joseline Peña-Melnyk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseline_Pe%C3%B1a-Melnyk), but opposition from State Senate President [Bill Ferguson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Ferguson_\(politician\)). In January 2026, the Governor's Redistricting Advisory Commission voted to recommend a congressional map that would redraw the 1st congressional district to make it more favorable for Democrats.[^cite_note-140] The map passed the state house on February 2.[^cite_note-MDmap-119] On February 20, Ferguson told reporters that the window to redraw Maryland's congressional maps had closed, killing the bill.[^cite_note-141] | Steady | Steady | Steady |
| [Missouri](#Missouri) | [Districts left in place](80917394.md) | The [Missouri Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Legislature) passed maps to create a new safely Republican seat in the [5th district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri's_5th_congressional_district). Governor [Mike Kehoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Kehoe) signed the map into law in September 2025.[^cite_note-\:1-111] The maps may be blocked by a referendum if enough signatures are gathered.[^cite_note-142] In March 2026, opponents of the map said they had enough signatures for the referendum to qualify.[^cite_note-Signatures-143] On August 4, 2026, Missouri Secretary of State [Denny Hoskins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denny_Hoskins) blocked the referendum from taking place, arguing that the referendum would be unconstitutional under [Missouri's Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Missouri),[^cite_note-144] a decision which was upheld by [Cole County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cole_County,_Missouri) Circuit Judge Daniel Green on August 19.[^cite_note-145] However, on September 3, the [Missouri Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Supreme_Court) unanimously ruled that the referendum is sufficient and must be put on the ballot, thus leaving the 2022 maps in place for the 2026 elections.[^cite_note-\:6-112] On September 8, U.S. Supreme Court Justice [Brett Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh) denied an emergency appeal by State Republicans.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] However, the same day, Chief U.S. District Judge [Stephen Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_R._Clark) of the [Eastern District of Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Eastern_District_of_Missouri) issued a [temporary restraining order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporary_restraining_order) in a separate lawsuit prohibiting the state from using anything but the new 2025 maps.[^cite_note-148] On September 10, the U.S. Supreme Court granted an emergency stay of Judge Clark's order, putting the 2022 map back into place for the 2026 elections.[^cite_note-149] However, on September 21, the [United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Eighth_Circuit), ruling on the merits, affirmed the temporary restraining order and remanded the case to the lower court for entry of a [permanent injunction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_injunction) enjoining Missouri to use the 2025 map, but stayed their ruling for a week pending appeal to the U.S. Supreme Court.[^cite_note-150] On September 25, the U.S. Supreme Court unanimously held that state law rules in the case and granted an emergency stay of the Eighth Circuit's order and Judge Clark's injunction, leaving the 2022 maps in place for the 2026 elections.[^cite_note-151] | Steady | Steady | Steady |
| [New York](#New_York) | Districts left in place | Voters in [NY-11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York's_11th_congressional_district) filed a lawsuit in October 2025, claiming the Staten Island-based district illegally dilutes the power of Black and Hispanic voters in the district.[^cite_note-152] Acting [New York Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Supreme_Court) Justice [Jeffrey Pearlman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Pearlman) ordered the [independent redistricting commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_redistricting_commission) to make new maps to reconfigure NY-11 by February 6, 2026.[^cite_note-NYMap-153] The deadline was suspended after [Rep. Malliotakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicole_Malliotakis), who represents the district, appealed the ruling.[^cite_note-154] On March 2, the U.S. Supreme Court temporarily blocked Pearlman's order, allowing the state's current map to be used for the 2026 election.[^cite_note-155] | Steady | Steady | Steady |
| [North Carolina](#North_Carolina) | [New districts enacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_North_Carolina_redistricting) | The state Senate passed the bill for a new map making the competitive 1st congressional district more Republican-leaning on October 21, 2025. The state House passed it the next day. The governor, Josh Stein, does not have the authority to veto the map.[^cite_note-NCmap-113] It has received pushback from the [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP) and [Common Cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Cause), both of which accused the new map of diluting the minority vote. A hearing was held on November 19 to consider blocking the map.[^cite_note-156] | Steady | Decrease 1 | Increase 1 |
| [Ohio](#Ohio) | New districts enacted | Ohio voters amended the constitution in 2018, creating a process that requires both Republicans and Democrats in the state legislature to vote on the new congressional map. In the 2020 redistricting cycle, Democrats did not back a Republican proposal, necessitating a new map to be drawn in 2025. In October 2025, the Ohio Redistricting Commission passed a compromise map in a unanimous vote. The new map makes the 1st district more competitive, the 9th district lean more Republican, and the 13th district lean more Democratic.[^cite_note-OHnewmap-106] | Steady | Decrease 2 | Increase 2 |
| [South Carolina](#South_Carolina) | Districts left in place | In June 2025, the [South Carolina Supreme Court](6985896.md) heard arguments in *[League of Women Voters v. Alexander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Women_Voters_of_South_Carolina_v._Alexander)*, as to whether the congressional maps are an unlawful partisan gerrymander in violation of the [state's constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_South_Carolina).[^cite_note-gm-157] In September 2025, the court ruled that partisan gerrymandering is not in violation of the state's constitution, repeatedly referencing the 2019 SCOTUS case *[Rucho v. Common Cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rucho_v._Common_Cause)*, leaving the current map in place.[^cite_note-\:0-101]Following the U.S. Supreme Court's decision in *[Louisiana v. Callais](80600781.md)*, Governor [Henry McMaster](3482397.md) called a special legislative session in May 2026 to consider redistricting and the state house approved a new map that would redraw the state's sole majority-minority district. However, the state senate ultimately voted against advancing the map after it became clear that passage would occur after the start of early voting in the state's primary election.[^cite_note-158] | Steady | Steady | Steady |
| [Tennessee](#Tennessee) | [New districts enacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Tennessee_redistricting) | In April 2026, the Supreme Court ruled on *[Louisiana v. Callais](80600781.md)* that stricter scrutiny had to be applied with regard to section 2 of the [Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act). After facing calls to redistrict from U.S. Senator [Marsha Blackburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsha_Blackburn) and U.S. President [Donald Trump](4848272.md), Governor [Bill Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Lee_\(Tennessee_politician\)) called a special session to redraw the sole majority-minority district in Tennessee.[^cite_note-159] | Decrease 1 | Steady | Increase 1 |
| [Texas](#Texas) | [New districts enacted](80621571.md) | In *League of United Latin American Citizens v. Abbott*, held from May to June 2025 in the [U.S. District Court for the Western District of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Western_District_of_Texas), plaintiffs argued that the congressional districts passed by the state legislature discriminated against Black and Latino voters in violation of the Voting Rights Act.[^cite_note-160]In early 2025, the Trump Administration urged Texas officials to redraw congressional districts. This was followed by a meeting of the Texas Republican congressional delegation to discuss mid-decade redistricting to secure extra Republican seats.[^cite_note-161] On July 9, Governor [Greg Abbott](1492735.md) called a special session of the legislature to be held later that month with the goal of creating 5 new Republican seats. After Texas House Democrats staged a weeks-long walkout, the legislature passed the new maps on August 23, and Abbott signed them into law on August 29.[^cite_note-tg-107] The previous case was paused, pending new maps.[^cite_note-LULAC-103] After passage, on August 27, the court scheduled a preliminary injunction hearing to begin on October 1 to October 10.[^cite_note-oh-162] On November 18, the panel issued a preliminary injunction blocking the new map, putting the former map back in place. The case on the 2021 maps remains open, pending action from the U.S. Supreme Court in *[Louisiana v. Callais](80600781.md)*.[^cite_note-\:5-108] On November 21, the U.S. Supreme Court issued an administrative stay on the lower court ruling, temporarily leaving the new map in place.[^cite_note-TXmap-109] On December 4, the Supreme Court ruled 6–3 in allowing the map to stand for the 2026 elections.[^cite_note-163] | Decrease 3 | Decrease 2 | Increase 5 |
| [Utah](#Utah) | New districts enacted | In July 2023, in *[League of Women Voters v. Utah State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Women_Voters_v._Utah_State_Legislature)*, the [Utah Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah_Supreme_Court) heard arguments alleging that the Utah Legislature violated [a citizen-passed anti-gerrymandering proposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Utah_Proposition_4) by dividing Salt Lake County voters into all four of Utah's districts. The case was sent back to a state district court, which held a hearing in January 2025. A ruling for the League of Women Voters was issued on August 25. New maps were expected to be drawn up by the Legislature by September 24 and finalized by November.[^cite_note-redrawn-164] On October 6, the Utah Legislature passed a new map, which changed 2 Republican districts to be more competitive.[^cite_note-\:2-165] On November 10, Utah Third District Judge Dianna Gibson ruled that the new map also violated Proposition 4, opting to approve a plaintiff-submitted alternative, Map 1, which creates a safe Democratic district comprising only [Salt Lake County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_Lake_County).[^cite_note-\:4-104] | Increase 1 | Steady | Decrease 1 |
| [Virginia](#Virginia) | Districts left in place | Democratic state lawmakers announced a plan to alter the state constitution to draw a new congressional map before the 2026 elections making 3-4 more Democratic seats.[^cite_note-auto-118] The proposed mechanism was an amendment to the [Constitution of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Virginia) allowing the [General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_General_Assembly) to modify the state's congressional districts if any other state did so between January 1, 2025 and October 31, 2030 for any reason other than normal decennial redistricting or to satisfy a court order. Redistricting was transferred from the General Assembly to a bipartisan commission in [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Virginia_Question_1), and the power would revert to the commission for the normal 2030 cycle. The amendment proposal first passed the General Assembly on October 31, 2025. The amendment procedure requires any proposal to pass in both houses of the General Assembly, then it must pass both houses again after the next [Virginia House of Delegates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_House_of_Delegates) election is held (every two years), then it must be approved in a referendum.[^cite_note-166] [Virginia Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Senate) minority leader [Ryan McDougle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryan_McDougle), a Republican, sued Democratic House Speaker [Don Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Scott_\(Virginia_politician\)) over the legality of the amendment. On January 27, 2026, [Tazewell County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tazewell_County,_Virginia) Circuit Court Judge Jack Hurley, Jr. ruled that the amendment could not satisfy the intervening-election requirement since early voting in the [2025 Virginia elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Virginia_elections) was underway at the time of passage.[^cite_note-167] On January 28, the [Democratic Party of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Virginia) appealed the ruling; the court of appeals, citing the importance of the case, forwarded it to the [Supreme Court of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Virginia) for an expedited ruling on February 4.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169] On February 10, the General Assembly passed the amendment proposal the required second time and included a draft map that would likely net Democrats four seats.[^cite_note-170] On February 13, the Supreme Court allowed the April 21 [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Virginia_redistricting_amendment) to go forward while it considered the matter. The referendum passed with 51.7% voting yes.[^cite_note-171] The next day, Judge Hurley ruled the referendum to be unconstitutional on the basis of another lawsuit and enjoined the state board of elections from certifying the result. Attorney General [Jay Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Jones) appealed the decision; the Supreme Court declined to stay it while the original case was ongoing and as a result the board was unable to certify the result at its May meeting.[^cite_note-172] [^cite_note-173] The Supreme Court heard oral arguments in the case on April 27.[^cite_note-174] On May 8, the Supreme Court invalidated the amendment in a 4–3 decision, with the majority agreeing with Judge Hurley's ruling that its first passage was too late to satisfy the intervening-election requirement.[^cite_note-175]Following the ruling, Speaker Scott and Attorney General Jones filed an emergency appeal to the [United States Supreme Court](31737.md).[^cite_note-176] On May 15, the Supreme Court denied the appeal.[^cite_note-177] | Steady | Steady | Steady |
| [Wisconsin](#Wisconsin) | Districts left in place | In July 2025, a lawsuit was filed by two liberal law firms in a Wisconsin district court, asking to consider the constitutionality of Wisconsin's congressional maps.[^cite_note-178] In September 2025, the Wisconsin Supreme court addressed this lawsuit and asked each party to provide briefs on whether this court case should proceed.[^cite_note-179] On March 31, 2026, a judicial panel dismissed the challenge.[^cite_note-180] | Steady | Steady | Steady |
| Net change (as of October 3, 2026) | Increase 1 | Decrease 13 | Increase 12 |  |  |

2. [California 38](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California's_38th_congressional_district)

6. [Texas 32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas's_32nd_congressional_district)

7. [Texas 35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas's_35th_congressional_district)

8. [Utah 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah's_1st_congressional_district)

### Seats with multiple incumbents running

The following districts will have multiple incumbent representatives running, a product of multiple districts merging in redistricting.

1. [California 40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California's_40th_congressional_district): [Ken Calvert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Calvert) (R) and [Young Kim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Kim) (R)

2. [Texas 18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas's_18th_congressional_district): [Christian Menefee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Menefee) (D) defeated [Al Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Green_\(politician\)) (D)

## References

[^cite_note-1]: &#x20;As well as five of the six [non-voting delegates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delegate_\(United_States_Congress\)).

[^cite_note-Independents1-2]: &#x20;[Kevin Kiley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Kiley_\(politician\)), who was re-elected as a Republican in 2024, is an [independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_Independent) who caucuses with the Republicans, and is not included in the number of seats held by Republicans.

[^cite_note-3]: &#x20;As a result of redistricting.

[^cite_note-4]: &#x20;As a result of death, resignation, or expulsion.

[^cite_note-83]: &#x20;Calculated by taking the difference of 100% and all other candidates combined.

[^cite_note-Davis-91]: &#x20;Davis was elected to a previous, competitive, version of this district which Trump only won by 3.2 points in 2024, but it was redrawn to be more strongly Republican-leaning in 2025 by the North Carolina state legislature.

[^cite_note-Kaptur-92]: &#x20;Kaptur was first elected to a previous, competitive but Republican-tilting, version of this district which Trump won by 6.7 points in 2024, but it was redrawn to be more strongly Republican-leaning by a bipartisan redistricting commission, as the previous maps expired in 2024.

[^cite_note-Cuellar-93]: &#x20;Cuellar was elected to a previous, competitive but Republican-tilting, version of this district which Trump only won by 7.3 points in 2024, but it was redrawn to be more strongly Republican-leaning in 2025 by the Texas state legislature.

[^cite_note-Gonzalez-94]: &#x20;Gonzalez was elected to a previous, competitive but Republican-tilting, version of this district which Trump only won by 4.4 points in 2024, but it was redrawn to be more strongly Republican-leaning in 2025 by the Texas state legislature.

[^cite_note-kind-96]: &#x20;Kiley was originally elected as a Republican. He left the Republican Party to become an independent in March 2026 but remains a member of the [House Republican Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Republican_Conference).

[^cite_note-change-123]: &#x20;Competitive seats are defined as seats won by less than 10 points by their respective party in 2024

[^cite_note-220]: &#x20;The 2026 edition of the Cook PVI includes redistricting changes in Missouri that were struck down by the Missouri Supreme Court in September 2026.<sup>[\[208\]](#cite_note-219)</sup>

[^cite_note-227]: &#x20;Van Drew was first elected as a Democrat but switched to the Republican Party in December 2019.

[^cite_note-234]: &#x20;In Ohio, congressional candidates file with the board of elections of the most populous county in their districts, not with the Secretary of State.<sup>[\[220\]](#cite_note-OH2026-233)</sup>
